=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a tree with N vertices.
The vertices are numbered 1, 2, \ldots, N.
The i-th edge (1\leq i\leq N-1) connects vertices U_i and V_i, with a length of L_i.
For each K=1,2,\ldots, N, solve the following problem.

Takahashi and Aoki play a game. The game proceeds as follows.

- First, Aoki specifies K distinct vertices on the tree.
- Then, Takahashi constructs a walk that starts and ends at vertex 1, and passes through all the vertices specified by Aoki.

The score is defined as the length of the walk constructed by Takahashi. Takahashi wants to minimize the score, while Aoki wants to maximize it.
Find the score when both players play optimally.


Definition of a walk
    A walk on an undirected graph (possibly a tree) is a sequence of k vertices and k-1 edges v_1,e_1,v_2,\ldots,v_{k-1},e_{k-1},v_k (where k is a positive integer)
    such that edge e_i connects vertices v_i and v_{i+1}. The same vertex or edge can appear multiple times in the sequence.  
    A walk is said to pass through vertex x if there exists at least one i (1\leq i\leq k) such that v_i=x. (There can be multiple such i.)  
    The walk is said to start and end at v_1 and v_k, respectively, and the length of the walk is the sum of the lengths of e_1, e_2, \ldots, e_{k-1}.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
U_1 V_1 L_1
U_2 V_2 L_2
\vdots
U_{N-1} V_{N-1} L_{N-1}

Output

Print N lines.
The i-th line (1\leq i\leq N) should contain the answer to the problem for K=i.

Constraints


- 2\leq N\leq 2\times 10^5
- 1\leq U_i<V_i\leq N
- 1\leq L_i\leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.
- The given graph is a tree.

Sample Input 1

5
1 2 3
2 3 5
2 4 2
1 5 3

Sample Output 1

16
22
26
26
26

For K=1, Aoki's optimal move is to specify vertex 3, and Takahashi's optimal move is to construct a path vertex 1 \to vertex 2 \to vertex 3 \to vertex 2 \to vertex 1, resulting in a score of 16.
For K=2, Aoki's optimal move is to specify vertices 3 and 5, and Takahashi's optimal move is to construct a path such as vertex 1 \to vertex 5 \to vertex 1 \to vertex 2 \to vertex 3 \to vertex 2 \to vertex 1, resulting in a score of 22.
For K\geq 3, the score when both players play optimally is 26.

Sample Input 2

3
1 2 1000000000
2 3 1000000000

Sample Output 2

4000000000
4000000000
4000000000

Beware that the answer may not fit in a 32-bit integer.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now need compute Δ_root efficiently for N up to 2e5. The recursive merging of sequences could be O(N^2) if naively merging sorted lists. Need use small-to-large with heaps? Let's understand.

At each node, we need combine children's marginal sequences with modification: for each child, add edge weight to first element of child's sequence. Then merge all sequences sorted descending. Need produce Δ_v as sorted list/multiset of all marginal gains in subtree. This is analogous to maintaining a max-heap of marginal gains; but modification "add edge weight to maximum element of child's heap" is key. We can process postorder with a priority queue per subtree. For node v, start with? For leaf, heap contains [0]. For internal, for each child c with heap H_c representing Δ_c sorted descending. We need transform H_c: pop its maximum (Δ_c[1]) and push max + w_c back? Wait B_c = [w_c + Δ_c[1]] + rest. Since Δ_c[1] is the largest element in H_c. We can update H_c by replacing its maximum element with max + w_c. But careful: If heap is max-heap, Δ_c sorted descending. The largest element is Δ_c[1]. Need add w_c to it. This increases it, so it remains largest. Then merge H_c into H_v. For leaf, H=[0], add parent edge to max gives [w]. This matches.

Now, let's think about memory and implementation details.

Leftist heap Node:
class Node:
    __slots__ = ('val','left','right','dist')
    def __init__(self, val):
        self.val = val
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.dist = 1

Meld function:
def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    if a.val < b.val:
        a, b = b, a
    a.right = meld(a.right, b)
    if not a.left:
        # dist left 0? Need compare
        if a.right is not None:
            a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left
        # if both? Let's use dist function
    if (a.left.dist if a.left else 0) < (a.right.dist if a.right else 0):
        a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left
    a.dist = (a.right.dist if a.right else 0) + 1
    return a

Need be careful: dist of leaf should be 1. If right None, dist=1. If both children None, dist=1. Good.

When melding, if a.val == b.val, no swap; okay.

Potential recursion depth: leftist heap right path length <= log2(size+1)? With dist property, right path length O(log n). Meld recurses down right path of a, swapping as needed. So depth O(log n). Good.

But note: We call meld for every child, and the heap might be large. The recursion depth of a single meld is O(log(size)), safe. However, if we use recursive meld inside a recursive DFS, total recursion? We'll process tree iteratively, so only meld recursion. Need set recursionlimit maybe 1e6 anyway.

for v in order:

        if to == parent[v]:
            continue
        parent[to] = v
        pw[to] = w
        order.append(to)

Then process reversed(order):
heaps = [None]*(N+1)
for v in reversed(order):
    h = None
    for to,w in adj[v]:
        if parent[to] == v:
            ch = heaps[to]
            ch.val += w
            h = meld(h, ch)
    if h is None:
        h = Node(0)
    heaps[v] = h

This loops adjacency again. For leaf, h None, Node(0). For internal, h non-None. But what if an internal node has children but all child heaps? yes.

Before final, let's deeply verify the mathematical reduction and algorithm to avoid hidden flaw.

Detailed proof of Δ_v:
Define F_v(k) = maximum total length of union of paths from v to k leaves in subtree of v, not counting any edge above v. For leaf, F_v(1)=0. For internal with children c_i, F_v(k)=max_{Σk_i=k} Σ (if k_i>0 then w_i + F_{c_i}(k_i) else 0). Let Δ_v(k)=F_v(k)-F_v(k-1) for k=1..L_v. For leaf Δ=[0]. For child c, define G_c(k)=0 if k=0 else w_c+F_c(k). Its marginal sequence is [w_c+Δ_c(1), Δ_c(2), ...]. Since Δ_c is nonincreasing, this is nonincreasing. Then F_v is the max convolution of G_c. For separable concave functions, the marginal sequence of the max convolution is the sorted merge of the marginal sequences. This can be shown by greedy allocation: At each additional leaf, choose the child with the largest next marginal; because each child's next marginal sequence is nonincreasing, this greedy yields optimal for every k. Thus Δ_v is sorted merge. Good.

Now, leftist heap stores Δ_v. Adding w_c to root (max) transforms Δ_c into G_c's marginal sequence. Meld merges children. Good.

Potential issue: If a child subtree has multiple leaves, Δ_c sequence length L_c. G_c marginal sequence length L_c. The first marginal w_c+Δ_c[1] may be larger than Δ_c[1] but what about if Δ_c[1] is not root after previous edge additions? It is root. Good.

Now, let's consider if edge weights are large and root increment can make a marginal bigger than all others; leftist heap root val += w works. If we store Node.val as int, no overflow.

Now, let's think about performance of leftist heap with Python recursion and object attributes. 2e5 nodes, each edge one meld. Meld recursion depth O(log n), but number of recursive calls per meld maybe right path length. In a leftist heap, right path length is O(log n), but meld may swap and recurse on right child. So total calls maybe O(N log N) ~ 3.6M. Each call creates no Node, just attribute access. Should be okay. But we also have DFS order and adjacency loops. AtCoder Python with 2e5 maybe okay if optimized.

Could we avoid leftist heap and use "heapq small-to-large" to be safer? Let's compare code complexity. Leftist heap code is not too long. But recursive meld function with __slots__ and dist. Need ensure no recursion limit issue. We'll set sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000). Meld recursion depth O(log N), but if leftist property broken? It won't. However, if we add to root, dist unchanged, property holds. Good.

Potential issue with leftist heap meld and root increment: We add w to child heap root before melding. But if the child heap root is not the maximum because we previously added to root and then melded? Wait heap invariant: root is max. Meld ensures root max. Adding to root increases, so root remains max. Good.

Let's prove: The set of edges that must be traversed at least once in a closed walk from root visiting S is exactly the minimal subtree connecting root and S. In a tree, any walk from root to root visiting a vertex v must traverse each edge on path root-v an even number? Actually if an edge is on path to v, to go from root side to v side and eventually return to root, you must cross it at least twice (down and up). Could cross more. So lower bound 2*union. Achievable by DFS traversal of the minimal subtree starting/ending at root, traversing each edge twice. Yes. So score = 2 union.

Now, let's think about input reading. Use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` for speed. Parse ints. N first, then 3*(N-1) ints. We'll build adjacency. Code:
import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it)
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    total = 0
    for _ in range(N-1):
        u = next(it); v = next(it); l = next(it)
        adj[u].append((v,l))
        adj[v].append((u,l))
        total += l
...

    h = None

            h = meld(h, ch)

    heaps[v] = h

Now, let's consider if the leftist heap meld recursion could hit Python recursion limit if the heap is not leftist due to a bug in dist update. Let's verify dist update:
def meld(a,b):
    if a is None: return b
    if b is None: return a
    if a.val < b.val:
        a,b = b,a
    a.right = meld(a.right, b)
    if (a.left.dist if a.left else 0) < (a.right.dist if a.right else 0):
        a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left
    a.dist = (a.right.dist if a.right else 0) + 1
    return a
Suppose a.left None, a.right None after meld? If b None? But b not None. After a.right = meld(None,b) = b. Then dist left 0, dist right maybe >=1, swap, so a.left=b, a.right=None. dist=1. Good.
Suppose a.left has dist 2, a.right has dist 1; no swap, dist=2. Right path length 2. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can simplify by using `heapq` and small-to-large to avoid custom leftist heap. Which is more likely to pass? Let's evaluate performance more concretely.

Leftist heap:
- Each edge triggers one meld call. But meld is recursive. For N=2e5, if tree is balanced, each meld of two heaps of size m may have recursion depth O(log m). Total recursive calls maybe O(N log N) ~ 3.6M. Each call is Python function call, attribute access. 3.6M calls maybe okay (~1s? Actually Python function call ~100ns? No, ~100-200ns? More like 0.1us? 3.6M ~0.4s? But with attribute access maybe 1-2s). Plus building. Should pass.

But worst-case for leftist heap meld total calls? In a leftist heap, meld cost is O(log n) per meld. Sum over edges O(N log N). For a chain, each node has one child, meld(h, ch) where h is None? Wait if a node has one child, h starts None, ch=child heap, h=ch after adding edge. We do not call meld because h is None? In our loop, for each child:
if h is None? We don't special-case; we call h = meld(h, ch). If h is None, meld returns ch immediately O(1). So chain O(N). For a node with multiple children, meld cost. Balanced tree: at each internal node, meld children heaps. The total meld cost maybe O(N log N). Good.

However, leftist heap meld recursion depth is O(log n), but if we meld a large heap with a small heap, recursion goes down right path of large heap, O(log n). Good.

Small-to-large heapq:
- Each element moved O(log N) times, each heappush C function but Python call to heapq.heappush. 3.6M C calls maybe okay. But also if we use `heapq.heappush` inside Python loop, each call is Python function that invokes C? Actually `heapq.heappush` is a Python function? Let's check: In CPython, heapq.py imports from _heapq import heappush, so heappush is built-in C function. So call overhead C? It's a built-in function, call from Python. 3.6M calls okay. But total element moves maybe more than N log N? Let's compute worst-case for small-to-large with heaps:
At each node, merge smaller into larger. Each time an element moves, the size of the heap containing it at least doubles. So <= log2(N) moves. So <=3.6M for N=2e5. Each move heappush O(log n) in C, but heappush is C and does comparisons of Python ints. 3.6M*~18=65M comparisons in C, maybe okay. Leftist heap does 3.6M Python recursive calls, each with comparisons and attribute assignments. Which is faster? Hard to know. Leftist heap may be more elegant and guaranteed O(N log N) but Python recursion overhead. Small-to-large heapq uses C for heap operations but more operations. Both likely pass. Leftist heap code is accepted in many AtCoder solutions.

But there is a subtle issue with small-to-large heapq: The operation `heap[0] -= w` for max-heap stored as negative. If heap[0] is the most negative, adding w to value means `heap[0] -= w` (e.g., -8 -> -13 for +5). This keeps it the minimum. Good. But if we use `heapq` min-heap, merging smaller into larger by heappush each element. We need to add edge weight to child heap root before merging. If child heap is the larger and we choose it as base, we add to its root. If child heap is smaller, we add to its root then push all elements. Good.

Could small-to-large heapq be simpler and less risky than leftist heap? Let's compare code:

Actually ints are immutable; the list ch holds references to ints. When heappush into h, it appends the same int object reference. So the int object is referenced by both lists. If we leave ch, memory for list ch remains (references), but ints shared. This could double list overhead for heaps that have been merged, because `heaps[child]` still references the old list, and h references the same ints plus maybe reordered. Actually each time an element moves, a reference to the same int is appended to a new list; the old list still holds the reference. If we don't clear old lists, an element may be referenced in many old lists along its move path, leading to O(N log N) list slots memory! That could be high. Memory with clearing: Each element's int object is referenced by exactly one active heap list (plus maybe old empty lists). The int objects are created once? Before final, let's maybe think of an alternative "slope trick with priority queues" using heapq small-to-large. Which code is more robust? Let's compare risk of leftist heap recursion vs heapq small-to-large memory/time.

Small-to-large heapq code:
```
import sys, heapq

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    total = 0
    for _ in range(N-1):
        u,v,l = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append((v,l))
        adj[v].append((u,l))
        total += l
    parent = [0]*(N+1)
    parent[1] = -1
    order = []
    stack = [1]
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        order.append(v)
        for to,w in adj[v]:
            if to == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[to] = v
            stack.append(to)
    heaps = [None]*(N+1)
    for v in reversed(order):
        h = None
        for to,w in adj[v]:
            if parent[to] == v:
                ch = heaps[to]
                ch[0] -= w
                if h is None:
                    h = ch
                else:
                    if len(h) < len(ch):
                        h, ch = ch, h
                    for x in ch:
                        heapq.heappush(h, x)
                    ch.clear()
        if h is None:
            h = [0]
        heaps[v] = h
    vals = [-x for x in heaps[1]]
    vals.sort(reverse=True)
    ...
```
Need check `ch[0] -= w` when ch is a heap of negative values. Example leaf [0], w=5 -> ch[0] = -5, value 5. Good. But if we store negative values, the max positive is min negative. Adding w to max value means negative decreases by w. So `ch[0] -= w`. Good.
When merging, if h is None, h=ch. But if h is not None and len(h) < len(ch), swap. Then for x in ch: heappush(h,x). But note `ch` is a heap list; after we push all its elements into h, we clear ch. However, if h and ch are the same list? We ensure not. But in the swap case, h is original ch (current child heap), ch is original h (previous heap). We push previous heap elements into current child heap. This is okay. But wait, we already added edge weight to current child heap's root (`ch[0] -= w`) before swap. If we swap, h is current child heap with modified root. Good. We then push previous h elements into it. Good.
But there is a subtle issue: If h is not None and len(h) >= len(ch), we push ch elements into h. After pushing, we clear ch. But if ch is a heap that is also referenced by `heaps[to]` for the current child, clearing it is fine. However, if ch is a heap that is also referenced by `heaps[some previous child]` and we clear it, fine. But what if ch is the same object as `h` due to previous alias? Let's see: h may be a heap from a previous child. If previous child's heap was merged into h, and we cleared the smaller, h is the larger. Could h also be referenced by `heaps[previous_child]` if previous child was the larger and we didn't clear it. Then h and heaps[previous_child] are same list. Later, if we swap h,ch, ch could be that same list? In the swap case, h is current child heap, ch is previous h. We clear ch, which is also heaps[previous_child]. Fine. But if we don't swap, h remains previous h, ch is current child heap. We push current child heap into previous h and clear current child heap. Fine. No duplicate active list. Good.

However, there is a potential issue with `ch.clear()` if `ch` is the same as `h`? Not possible because we only merge if h is not None and we don't set h=ch. In swap, h and ch are different lists. Good.

But wait, when h is None, h=ch. Then heaps[v] = h. If later at parent, this heap is a child heap. It may be merged into parent's h. If it is the larger, it becomes h and is not cleared; heaps[v] and parent's h reference same list. If later at parent, another child heap is merged and parent's h is swapped? Suppose parent has two children: child A heap size 10, child B heap size 5. Process A: h=None -> h=heaps[A]. Process B: ch=heaps[B], add root, len(h)=10 >= len(ch)=5, push B into h, clear heaps[B]. h remains heaps[A]. At grandparent, heaps[parent] = h (same as heaps[A]). If grandparent has another child C with heap size 20, then at grandparent, h=None? Actually grandparent processes its children. Suppose first child is parent (heap size15), h=heaps[parent]. Then child C heap size20: ch=heaps[C], add root, len(h)=15 < len(ch)=20, swap: h=heaps[C], ch=heaps[parent]. Push ch (parent heap) into h (C heap), clear ch. Now heaps[parent] becomes empty, but h is heaps[C]. This is okay. The elements from parent heap are now in C heap. No active duplicates. Good.

Memory with clearing is O(N) list slots. But note: When we push elements from ch into h, the int objects are shared; after clearing ch, the ints are only in h. Good.

Time: Each element moves O(log N) times. But when we clear ch, we lose the heap structure of ch; no issue. However, if we push elements one by one from ch into h, the order of iteration over ch is arbitrary heap order, not sorted. heappush handles. Good.

Could we further optimize by using `h.extend(ch); heapq.heapify(h)` when ch is much smaller? We decided no due to O(size h). But maybe if we always extend smaller into larger and heapify larger, the cost is O(size h) each merge, which in a comb tree is O(N^2). So no.

Could we use `heapq.merge`? no.

Small-to-large heapq with clearing is simpler and uses C heappush. But total heappush calls 3.6M, each heappush O(log h) in C. 3.6M calls to C function from Python might be okay. Leftist heap has 3.6M Python recursive calls. Which is faster? Let's estimate: A Python function call ~0.1 micro? Actually Python function call ~100 ns? No, Python function call around 100-200 ns? Let's recall: Python can do ~10-20 million simple function calls per second? Maybe 50 ns? Hmm. 3.6M calls ~0.2s? But with attribute access and recursion maybe 1s. heappush C call 3.6M ~0.3s plus comparisons. Both okay.

But small-to-large heapq has a hidden O(N log^2 N) due to heappush log, but in C. Leftist heap O(N log N) in Python. I'd lean leftist heap for theoretical cleanliness. However, leftist heap recursion and object manipulation might be more error-prone but we've verified.

Let's think about if leftist heap can be implemented iteratively to avoid recursion and maybe faster. There is an iterative leftist heap meld algorithm:
1. Merge right paths of a and b, then fix leftist property on way back.
But recursive is simpler.

Could use "skew heap" with recursive meld and no dist, but worst-case recursion depth? Amortized but maybe okay. Leftist safer.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the `Node` class and `dist` if a node is a leaf and we add edge weight to its val; dist remains 1. Good.

Let's draft leftist heap code:

```
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1 << 25)

class Node:
    __slots__ = ('val', 'left', 'right', 'dist')
    def __init__(self, val):
        self.val = val
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.dist = 1

def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    if a.val < b.val:
        a, b = b, a
    a.right = meld(a.right, b)
    if (a.left.dist if a.left else 0) < (a.right.dist if a.right else 0):
        a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left
    a.dist = (a.right.dist if a.right else 0) + 1
    return a

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N_line = input()
    if not N_line:
        return
    N = int(N_line)
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N+1)]
    total = 0
    for _ in range(N-1):
        u, v, l = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append((v, l))
        adj[v].append((u, l))
        total += l

    parent = [0]*(N+1)
    parent[1] = -1
    order = []
    stack = [1]
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        order.append(v)
        for to, _ in adj[v]:
            if to == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[to] = v
            stack.append(to)

    heaps = [None]*(N+1)
    for v in reversed(order):
        h = None
        for to, w in adj[v]:
            if parent[to] == v:
                ch = heaps[to]
                ch.val += w
                h = meld(h, ch)
        if h is None:
            h = Node(0)
        heaps[v] = h

    root_heap = heaps[1]
    vals = []
    st = [root_heap]
    while st:
        x = st.pop()
        vals.append(x.val)
        if x.left is not None:
            st.append(x.left)
        if x.right is not None:
            st.append(x.right)

    vals.sort(reverse=True)
    L = len(vals)
    pref = [0]*(L+1)
    for i, x in enumerate(vals, 1):
        pref[i] = pref[i-1] + x

    out = []
    for K in range(1, N+1):
        if K <= L:
            ans = 2 * pref[K]
        else:
            ans = 2 * total
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write('\n'.join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check if `parent[to] == v` in processing reversed works for root's children. Yes. But in building order, we use `for to, _ in adj[v]` and `if to == parent[v]: continue`. Since no visited, but what about a node's neighbor that is not parent but already has parent set due to being discovered from another path? In a tree, no. But consider root with two children; when processing root, push both. Suppose stack LIFO: push child2 then child1? Actually adjacency order. Process child1, set parent of its children. No cross. Good.

However, there is a subtle issue: In the DFS stack, because we don't mark visited, when processing root, we push all neighbors. Then process one neighbor, say 2. In 2's adjacency, it sees root (parent) skip, and its children. But what if 2 has a neighbor 3 that was also pushed as a child of root? That would mean edge 2-3 and root-3, cycle. Not tree. Good.

Let's estimate leftist heap operations more concretely. Number of meld calls at top level = N-1? Actually for each edge, we call meld once in the loop (including h None). But recursive calls inside meld. In a balanced binary tree, at each internal node, melding two child heaps of size m and m: meld cost O(log m) ~ log m. Number of such nodes N/m, total over levels sum (N/m * log m) ~ N * sum_{level} log(size)/size? Wait for balanced tree, at level with subtrees size s, number nodes N/s, each meld cost log s, total (N/s) log s. Sum over s powers of 2 from 1 to N: N * sum log s / s. Sum log s / s converges? Let's compute: s=2^d, log s = d, number of nodes at that level N/2^d, cost per node d, total N * d / 2^d. Sum over d=1..logN d/2^d < 2. So total recursive calls O(N)! Wait that's surprising. But meld cost O(log s) and number of melds at level N/s, product N log s / s, sum converges to O(N). For a balanced tree, total meld work O(N). For a star, each meld of size1 with size k? Actually root melds leaves one by one: first h=leaf, second meld size1 with size1 cost O(1), third meld size2 with size1 cost O(log2), etc. Sum over k=1..N log k = O(N log N). So worst-case O(N log N). For N=2e5, ~3.6M recursive calls maybe. Good.

But is the sum of meld costs over all nodes O(N log N) worst-case? Yes, each edge meld cost O(log size), and size can be large for many edges in a "comb" where each node has one large child and one leaf. At each node, meld large heap size m with leaf size1 cost O(log m). There are O(N) such nodes, sum log m = O(N log N). So ~3.6M. Good.

3.6M recursive calls with simple operations likely okay in PyPy maybe ~1-2s. Plus sorting. Should pass.

Small-to-large heapq in a comb: each leaf element? Actually at each node, merge leaf size1 into large heap size m by pushing one element, cost O(log m), same O(N log N) C heappush. Similar. Leftist heap Python recursion maybe comparable.

Could optimize leftist heap by using iterative meld to avoid recursion overhead? Not necessary.

Could we use "skew heap" with no dist and simpler/faster? Skew heap meld:
```
def meld(a,b):
    if not a: return b
    if not b: return a
    if a.val < b.val: a,b=b,a
    a.right = meld(a.right,b)
    a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left
    return a
```
No dist, no conditional. But amortized O(log n), recursion depth can be O(n) in worst case? Skew heap is amortized but a single meld can have depth O(n) if the heap is skewed. However, the amortized bound is O(log n), but worst-case recursion depth could be O(n), causing recursion limit. Is that possible with our sequence of melds and root increments? Skew heap can become unbalanced; recursion depth might be large. Leftist heap safer.

Could implement leftist heap iteratively to avoid recursion and maybe speed? Let's think if needed.

Iterative leftist heap meld algorithm:
- Split the right paths of a and b, merge them by value, then rebuild.
Pseudo:
```
def meld(a,b):
    if not a: return b
    if not b: return a
    if a.val < b.val: a,b=b,a
    stack=[]
    while a and b:
        if a.val < b.val: a,b=b,a
        stack.append(a)
        a = a.right
    h = a or b
    while stack:
        x = stack.pop()
        x.right = h
        if dist(x.left) < dist(x.right): swap
        x.dist = dist(x.right)+1
        h = x
    return h
```
But need be careful: We need merge right paths of a and b. The standard recursive meld can be simulated with a stack of nodes whose right child will be updated. Let's derive.

Recursive meld(a,b):
if a.val < b.val swap.
a.right = meld(a.right,b)
fix a.
return a.

Iterative:
We want to follow the right path of the larger-root tree, swapping when b.root larger. We can maintain a stack of nodes on the right path that will be rebuilt from bottom up.
```
def meld(a,b):
    if a is None: return b
    if b is None: return a
    if a.val < b.val:
        a,b = b,a
    stack = []
    while True:
        if a.val < b.val:
            a,b = b,a
        stack.append(a)
        a = a.right
        if a is None:
            a = b
            break
        if b is None:
            break
    h = a
    while stack:
        x = stack.pop()
        x.right = h
        if dist(x.left) < dist(x.right):
            x.left, x.right = x.right, x.left
        x.dist = dist(x.right) + 1
        h = x
    return h
```
Need check when b becomes None: In recursive, if b is None, meld(a.right,None) returns a.right, so no change; then fix nodes. In iterative, if b is None, we break with h = a (the remaining right path). But we have already appended nodes? Let's test.

At loop start, we compare and maybe swap, append a, set a=a.right. If a is None, h=b, break. If b is None? b could become None only if we set? We never set b to None except maybe if a.right is None and we set a=b? Wait in recursive, b is a heap; it doesn't become None unless the original b was None. In our loop, b remains the smaller heap until it is attached. We don't update b. So b is never None except initial. The loop should continue until a is None, then attach b. But what about the case where a.right is not None and b is smaller; recursive meld(a.right,b) continues. So loop continues. We don't need check b None. If b is None initial, handled. So:
```
while a is not None:
    if a.val < b.val:
        a,b = b,a
    stack.append(a)
    a = a.right
h = b
while stack: ...
```
But if we swap, the old a becomes b, and new a is old b. We append new a, then move to its right. This matches recursive: if a.val < b.val swap, then recurse on a.right with b. Good.
However, if we swap, the node we append is the larger root. Then a = a.right (right child of larger root). b is the smaller root (old larger if swapped? Actually if swapped, b becomes old a, which is the smaller root; then recursive meld(a.right, b) where b is old a. Yes.)
If no swap, b remains smaller root. Good.

Let's refine iterative meld:

```
def dist(x):
    return x.dist if x else 0

def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    path = []
    while a is not None:
        if a.val < b.val:
            a, b = b, a
        path.append(a)
        a = a.right
    h = b
    while path:
        x = path.pop()
        x.right = h
        if (x.left.dist if x.left else 0) < (x.right.dist if x.right else 0):
            x.left, x.right = x.right, x.left
        x.dist = (x.right.dist if x.right else 0) + 1
        h = x
    return h
```

Need verify with case where b is None? handled. But in loop, b is never None. However, what if a is not None and b is None? handled at start. Good.

Iterative meld code:
```
def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    path = []
    while a is not None:
        if a.val < b.val:
            a, b = b, a
        path.append(a)
        a = a.right
    h = b
    while path:
        x = path.pop()
        x.right = h
        ld = x.left.dist if x.left else 0
        rd = x.right.dist if x.right else 0
        if ld < rd:
            x.left, x.right = x.right, x.left
            rd = ld  # after swap, right dist is old left dist
        x.dist = rd + 1
        h = x
    return h
```
Wait after swap, `x.right` becomes old left, so right dist = ld. Then `x.dist = rd + 1` where rd is old left dist. If no swap, right dist remains rd. So we can set `x.dist = (x.right.dist if x.right else 0) + 1` after swap to avoid confusion. Simpler:
```
if ld < rd:
    x.left, x.right = x.right, x.left
x.dist = (x.right.dist if x.right else 0) + 1
```
This computes right dist again. Fine.

Recursive meld:
```
meld(a,b):
    if a is None: return b
    if b is None: return a
    if a.val < b.val: swap(a,b)
    a.right = meld(a.right, b)
    if dist(a.left) < dist(a.right): swap children
    a.dist = dist(a.right)+1
    return a
```
The recursion follows the right spine of `a` (after possible swaps). We can simulate by storing the nodes on the right spine that will have their right child updated. At each step, choose the larger root between current `a` and `b`, push it, and continue with its right child and the other root. When current `a` becomes None, the remaining heap is `b`; then pop the stack and set each node's right child to the previously rebuilt heap, fix leftist property. This is exactly what iterative does. Good.

Let's test with a=5, b=10. At start a.val<b swap -> a=10,b=5. path=[10], a=10.right. Suppose 10.right=None, h=5, rebuild pop10 right=5, left maybe? If 10.left maybe 7, dist left1, right1, no swap, dist=2. Result root10 left7 right5. Heap property. Recursive meld(5,10): swap -> a=10,b=5, a.right=meld(None,5)=5, dist left1, right1, no swap, dist2. Now, if we use iterative meld, no need `sys.setrecursionlimit` for meld, but still no recursion. We can set anyway. Iterative code maybe slightly longer but robust. Let's decide final code with iterative meld to avoid recursion overhead? The final code can be:

```
def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    path = []
    while a is not None:
        if a.val < b.val:
            a, b = b, a
        path.append(a)
        a = a.right
    h = b
    while path:
        x = path.pop()
        x.right = h
        if (x.left.dist if x.left else 0) < (x.right.dist if x.right else 0):
            x.left, x.right = x.right, x.left
        x.dist = (x.right.dist if x.right else 0) + 1
        h = x
    return h
```

Need consider if `path` list grows large; right path length O(log n), so small. Good.

Let's test iterative meld with a case where a.right path and b.right path both non-empty. Example A root10 right8 right6, B root9 right7. Recursive:
meld(10,9): a=10,b=9, a.right=meld(8,9). meld(8,9): swap -> a=9,b=8, a.right=meld(9.right=7,8). meld(7,8): swap -> a=8,b=7, a.right=meld(8.right=6,7). meld(6,7): swap -> a=7,b=6, a.right=meld(7.right=None,6)=6, fix 7 left6, return7. Then fix8: right7, left? 8.left maybe None, swap left7, return8. Then fix9: right8, left? 9.left maybe None, swap left8, return9. Then fix10: right9, left? maybe None, swap left9, return10. Result root10 left9, 9.left8, 8.left7, 7.left6. Iterative:
a=10,b=9 path[10] a=8
a=8,b=9 swap -> a=9,b=8 path[10,9] a=9.right=7
a=7,b=8 swap -> a=8,b=7 path[10,9,8] a=8.right=6
a=6,b=7 swap -> a=7,b=6 path[10,9,8,7] a=7.right=None
h=6
rebuild pop7 right6, swap left6, h7
pop8 right7, swap left7, h8
pop9 right8, swap left8, h9
pop10 right9, swap left9, h10. Same. Good.

Now, let's decide final code with iterative meld. Let's write it cleanly:

```
class Node:
    __slots__ = ('val', 'left', 'right', 'dist')
    def __init__(self, val):
        self.val = val
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.dist = 1

def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    path = []
    while a is not None:
        if a.val < b.val:
            a, b = b, a
        path.append(a)
        a = a.right
    h = b
    while path:
        x = path.pop()
        x.right = h
        if (x.left.dist if x.left else 0) < (x.right.dist if x.right else 0):
            x.left, x.right = x.right, x.left
        x.dist = (x.right.dist if x.right else 0) + 1
        h = x
    return h
```

Let's write final code with recursive meld:

```
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)

class Node:
    __slots__ = ('val', 'left', 'right', 'dist')
    def __init__(self, val):
        self.val = val
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.dist = 1

def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    if a.val < b.val:
        a, b = b, a
    a.right = meld(a.right, b)
    if (a.left.dist if a.left else 0) < (a.right.dist if a.right else 0):
        a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left
    a.dist = (a.right.dist if a.right else 0) + 1
    return a
...
```

This is standard. I'll use this.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

sys.setrecursionlimit(1_000_000)


class Node:
    __slots__ = ("val", "left", "right", "dist")

    def __init__(self, val):
        self.val = val
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.dist = 1


def meld(a, b):
    if a is None:
        return b
    if b is None:
        return a
    if a.val < b.val:
        a, b = b, a

    a.right = meld(a.right, b)

    if (a.left.dist if a.left else 0) < (a.right.dist if a.right else 0):
        a.left, a.right = a.right, a.left

    a.dist = (a.right.dist if a.right else 0) + 1
    return a


def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline

    N = int(input())
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N + 1)]
    total_weight = 0

    for _ in range(N - 1):
        u, v, l = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append((v, l))
        adj[v].append((u, l))
        total_weight += l

    parent = [0] * (N + 1)
    parent[1] = -1
    order = []
    stack = [1]

    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        order.append(v)
        for to, _ in adj[v]:
            if to == parent[v]:
                continue
            parent[to] = v
            stack.append(to)

    heaps = [None] * (N + 1)

    for v in reversed(order):
        h = None

        for to, w in adj[v]:
            if parent[to] == v:
                ch = heaps[to]
                ch.val += w
                h = meld(h, ch)

        if h is None:
            h = Node(0)

        heaps[v] = h

    root_heap = heaps[1]

    vals = []
    st = [root_heap]

    while st:
        x = st.pop()
        vals.append(x.val)
        if x.left is not None:
            st.append(x.left)
        if x.right is not None:
            st.append(x.right)

    vals.sort(reverse=True)

    leaf_count = len(vals)
    pref = [0] * (leaf_count + 1)

    for i, x in enumerate(vals, 1):
        pref[i] = pref[i - 1] + x

    ans = []
    for k in range(1, N + 1):
        if k <= leaf_count:
            ans.append(str(2 * pref[k]))
        else:
            ans.append(str(2 * total_weight))

    print("\n".join(ans))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
